Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. I'm here because I've been feeling extremely down, sad, and just completely depressed for the past seven months. It feels like I can't shake this heavy, gloomy mood no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low mood consistently every single day for the past seven months, or have there been periods of a few weeks where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been like this nearly every single day for the past seven months. I haven't had any moments where I felt back to my normal, healthy self—it's just been a constant, heavy cloud.\n\nDoctor: During these past seven months, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost all of the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a loss of interest in my usual activities.\n\nDoctor: Since you haven't lost interest in your activities, have you been experiencing other physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, during this depression my appetite has dropped significantly and I've lost a lot of weight. But when my energy is high, I feel extremely restless and unable to sit still, like I'm constantly driven to move. I'm not really sure about any specific changes in my sleep patterns, though.\n\nDoctor: During those times when your energy is high and you feel restless, have you also found that you need much less sleep than usual, or that your thoughts are racing and you are talking much faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high-energy times, my thoughts spin uncontrollably and jump from one idea to another, and I talk so fast and constantly that others find it hard to interrupt me. As for my sleep, I'm not really sure and haven't paid close attention to how much sleep I've been getting during those periods.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, have these symptoms been severe enough to cause significant problems at work or in your social life, or perhaps required a hospital stay?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure about that, honestly. I haven't really thought about whether it caused major disruptions in my life, and I've never been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these high-energy states, do you find that you are still able to function at work or in your daily routine, or do these episodes clearly stand out as a noticeable change in your functioning to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, people close to me definitely notice a drastic change when I'm in those states. They often point out how incredibly fast I'm talking and how I constantly jump from one project to another. My family has also been really concerned about how impulsively I start spending money during those times.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of impulsive spending and racing thoughts, do you still manage to maintain your job responsibilities, or does your behavior cause you to miss work or get into trouble at your workplace?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about how it affects my job, to be honest. I haven't really thought about whether it has caused direct trouble at work or made me miss days."

The presence of clear social/familial noticeability of these high-energy periods confirms the presence of hypomanic or manic symptoms. The distinction between Bipolar I and II depends on the severity and functional impairment of the manic episodes (hospitalization or marked impairment), which remains unclear, so both remain in the differential.", "is_final": false}

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ hypomanic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Talkativeness: Speaking far more than usual and continuing to talk without rest.\n  - Rapid Speech: Speech speed is so fast that it is difficult for others to understand or interrupt.\n  - Pressured Speech: Cannot stop talking or tolerate silence due to an internal pressure to keep speaking.\n  - Racing Thoughts: Thoughts spinning too fast in the head, constantly jumping from one idea to another.\n  - Flooding of Thoughts: Too many thoughts emerging simultaneously, making them disorganized.\n  - Inability to Control Thoughts: Unable to stop or slow down thoughts and unable to calm the mind.\n  - Unrestrained Buying Sprees: Spending excessive amounts of money or shopping recklessly to a degree that creates financial distress for self and family.\n  - Foolish Business Investments: Making rash business investments or ventures without sufficient review.\n  - Sexual Indiscretions: Engaging in indiscriminate or risky sexual behavior that is out of character.\n  - Impulsive Risky Behavior: Excessively dangerous or foolish behavior, such as reckless driving or drug use, without considering the consequences.\n  Duration: 2 weeks\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Significant Weight Loss: Weight loss of more than 5% in a month without dieting.\n  - Weight Gain: Weight gain of more than 5% in a month.\n  - Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. I'm here because I've been feeling extremely down, sad, and just completely depressed for the past seven months. It feels like I can't shake this heavy, gloomy mood no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low mood consistently every single day for the past seven months, or have there been periods of a few weeks where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been like this nearly every single day for the past seven months. I haven't had any moments where I felt back to my normal, healthy self—it's just been a constant, heavy cloud.\n\nDoctor: During these past seven months, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost all of the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a loss of interest in my usual activities.\n\nDoctor: Since you haven't lost interest in your activities, have you been experiencing other physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, during this depression my appetite has dropped significantly and I've lost a lot of weight. But when my energy is high, I feel extremely restless and unable to sit still, like I'm constantly driven to move. I'm not really sure about any specific changes in my sleep patterns, though.\n\nDoctor: During those times when your energy is high and you feel restless, have you also found that you need much less sleep than usual, or that your thoughts are racing and you are talking much faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high-energy times, my thoughts spin uncontrollably and jump from one idea to another, and I talk so fast and constantly that others find it hard to interrupt me. As for my sleep, I'm not really sure and haven't paid close attention to how much sleep I've been getting during those periods.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, have these symptoms been severe enough to cause significant problems at work or in your social life, or perhaps required a hospital stay?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure about that, honestly. I haven't really thought about whether it caused major disruptions in my life, and I've never been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these high-energy states, do you find that you are still able to function at work or in your daily routine, or do these episodes clearly stand out as a noticeable change in your functioning to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, people close to me definitely notice a drastic change when I'm in those states. They often point out how incredibly fast I'm talking and how I constantly jump from one project to another. My family has also been really concerned about how impulsively I start spending money during those times.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of impulsive spending and racing thoughts, do you still manage to maintain your job responsibilities, or does your behavior cause you to miss work or get into trouble at your workplace?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about how it affects my job, to be honest. I haven't really thought about whether it has caused direct trouble at work or made me miss days.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall the longest period these high-energy episodes have lasted, and do you feel that you could have continued working normally if you had chosen to, or was it truly impossible to maintain your usual work performance?"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"Bipolar I Disorder\", \"Bipolar II Disorder\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. I'm here because I've been feeling extremely down, sad, and just completely depressed for the past seven months. It feels like I can't shake this heavy, gloomy mood no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this low mood consistently every single day for the past seven months, or have there been periods of a few weeks where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's been like this nearly every single day for the past seven months. I haven't had any moments where I felt back to my normal, healthy self—it's just been a constant, heavy cloud.\n\nDoctor: During these past seven months, have you lost interest or pleasure in almost all of the activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that, to be honest. I haven't really noticed a loss of interest in my usual activities.\n\nDoctor: Since you haven't lost interest in your activities, have you been experiencing other physical symptoms like significant changes in your appetite, sleep patterns, or feeling physically slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, during this depression my appetite has dropped significantly and I've lost a lot of weight. But when my energy is high, I feel extremely restless and unable to sit still, like I'm constantly driven to move. I'm not really sure about any specific changes in my sleep patterns, though.\n\nDoctor: During those times when your energy is high and you feel restless, have you also found that you need much less sleep than usual, or that your thoughts are racing and you are talking much faster than normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, during those high-energy times, my thoughts spin uncontrollably and jump from one idea to another, and I talk so fast and constantly that others find it hard to interrupt me. As for my sleep, I'm not really sure and haven't paid close attention to how much sleep I've been getting during those periods.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, have these symptoms been severe enough to cause significant problems at work or in your social life, or perhaps required a hospital stay?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure about that, honestly. I haven't really thought about whether it caused major disruptions in my life, and I've never been hospitalized for it.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these high-energy states, do you find that you are still able to function at work or in your daily routine, or do these episodes clearly stand out as a noticeable change in your functioning to those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, people close to me definitely notice a drastic change when I'm in those states. They often point out how incredibly fast I'm talking and how I constantly jump from one project to another. My family has also been really concerned about how impulsively I start spending money during those times.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of impulsive spending and racing thoughts, do you still manage to maintain your job responsibilities, or does your behavior cause you to miss work or get into trouble at your workplace?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about how it affects my job, to be honest. I haven't really thought about whether it has caused direct trouble at work or made me miss days.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall the longest period these high-energy episodes have lasted, and do you feel that you could have continued working normally if you had chosen to, or was it truly impossible to maintain your usual work performance?\n\nPatient: These high-energy periods typically last for about two weeks. During those times, far from being unable to work, I actually feel overflowing with energy and end up doing far more work than usual. I often find myself starting multiple projects and activities simultaneously because of how energized I am.\n\nDoctor: Besides never being hospitalized, have you ever experienced these high-energy episodes that lasted for an entire week or longer, or were they always shorter than that?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely last for a week or longer. In fact, these high-energy periods usually go on for about two weeks."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"diagnosis": "Bipolar I Disorder", "candidates": ["Bipolar I Disorder", "Bipolar II Disorder"], "reason": "The patient reports high-energy episodes characterized by racing thoughts, rapid speech, and impulsive spending that last for approximately two weeks. Because these episodes last for at least one week and represent a clear change in functioning, they meet the criteria for a manic episode, supporting a diagnosis of Bipolar I Disorder."}

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
